# Pi Coding Agent Overview

## What It Is

Pi Coding Agent is a minimal, extensible agent harness built by Earendil Inc. Rather than imposing a rigid workflow, it prioritizes customization—adapting to how users work instead of forcing users into predefined patterns. The platform is released under an MIT License and available via npm and other package managers.

## Core Architecture

Pi operates across four distinct modes: interactive (full terminal UI), print/JSON (script-friendly output), RPC (JSON protocol over stdin/stdout for non-Node integrations), and SDK (embeddable for custom applications). Sessions are structured as trees, enabling users to navigate, branch, and revisit previous conversation points within a single file.

## Key Features

**Model Flexibility:** The system supports 15+ AI providers—including Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, Azure, Bedrock, Mistral, Groq, and others. Users can switch models mid-session via `/model` command or cycle through favorites with keyboard shortcuts.

**Context Management:** Pi implements customizable context engineering through project-specific instruction files (AGENTS.md, SYSTEM.md), auto-compacting message history, and on-demand skill loading. As the documentation notes, the platform uses "a minimal system prompt" to maximize control over context window usage.

**Session Sharing:** Users can export conversations to HTML, upload to Gi
… 1,515 more characters

# Pi Agent Harness Repository Overview

## Repository Structure

Pi is organized as a **monorepo** with multiple focused packages under the `packages/` directory, complemented by configuration files, scripts, and documentation at the root level.

## Core Packages

The project contains four primary packages:

1. **@earendil-works/pi-coding-agent** — "Interactive coding agent CLI" that enables users to engage with an AI-powered development assistant through the command line.

2. **@earendil-works/pi-agent-core** — "Agent runtime with tool calling and state management" providing the foundational execution engine for agentic behavior.

3. **@earendil-works/pi-ai** — "Unified multi-provider LLM API" supporting OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, and additional language model providers through a consistent interface.

4. **@earendil-works/pi-telemetry** — Vendor-neutral telemetry infrastructure with "contracts, reference adapter, conformance tests, and typed schemas."

## Additional Packages

- **@earendil-works/pi-tui** — Terminal UI library featuring differential rendering capabilities for interactive console experiences.

## Key Directories

- `.github/` — GitHub-specific workflows and configurations
- `.husky/` — Git hook management
- `.pi/` — Project-specific configuration
- `packages/` — All npm packages and libraries
- `scripts/` — Build and utility scripts

## Technology Stack & Archi

# Pi Agent Harness: README Overview

## Project Description

Pi is a self-extensible coding agent project housed at pi.dev. The repository contains multiple interconnected packages for building AI-powered development tools.

## Core Packages

The project consists of five main packages:

1. **Pi Coding Agent** - An interactive CLI tool for coding tasks
2. **Pi Agent Core** - Runtime environment handling tool execution and state
3. **Pi AI** - Unified API supporting multiple LLM providers (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, etc.)
4. **Pi Telemetry** - Vendor-neutral telemetry with conformance testing
5. **Pi TUI** - Terminal UI library with differential rendering

## Key Architecture Notes

The documentation emphasizes that "Pi does not include a built-in permission system for restricting filesystem, process, network, or credential access." Users requiring stricter isolation should implement containerization using one of three documented patterns: Gondolin extension, plain Docker, or OpenShell sandbox.

## Development Workflow

Build commands include `npm run build`, `npm run check` for linting/formatting, and `./test.sh` for running tests. The project supports offline building via `npm run build:offline`.

## Supply Chain Security

The project implements strict dependency management practices including pinned exact versions for direct dependencies, lockfile verification, and scheduled s

=== ExtensionContext (lines 309-400) ===
export interface ExtensionContext {
	/** UI methods for user interaction */
	ui: ExtensionUIContext;
	/** Current run mode. Use "tui" to guard terminal-only UI such as custom components. */
	mode: ExtensionMode;
	/** Whether dialog-capable UI is available (true in TUI and RPC modes) */
	hasUI: boolean;
	/** Current working directory */
	cwd: string;
	/** Session manager (read-only) */
	sessionManager: ReadonlySessionManager;
	/** Model registry for API key resolution */
	modelRegistry: ModelRegistry;
	/** Current model (may be undefined) */
	model: Model<any> | undefined;
	/** Models scoped to this session (resolved from `--models` /
	 *  `enabledModels` settings against the available catalogue). Same set
	 *  the `/scoped-models` command shows. Empty when no scoping is
	 *  configured (all available models are usable). Read-only snapshot. */
	scopedModels: readonly ScopedModel[];
	/** Current thinking level, when provided by the session runtime. */
	thinkingLevel?: ThinkingLevel;
	/** Whether the agent is idle (not streaming) */
	isIdle(): boolean;

/**
 * Tool wrappers for extension-registered tools.
 *
 * These wrappers only adapt tool execution so extension tools receive the runner context.
 * Tool call and tool result interception is handled by AgentSession via agent-core hooks.
 */

import type { AgentTool } from "@earendil-works/pi-agent-core";
import { wrapToolDefinition } from "../tools/tool-definition-wrapper.ts";
import type { ExtensionRunner } from "./runner.ts";
import type { RegisteredTool } from "./types.ts";

/**
 * Wrap a RegisteredTool into an AgentTool.
 * Uses the runner's createContext() for consistent context across tools and event handlers.
 */
export function wrapRegisteredTool(registeredTool: RegisteredTool, runner: ExtensionRunner): AgentTool {
	const tool = wrapToolDefinition(registeredTool.definition, () => runner.createContext());
	const execute = tool.execute;
	return {
		...tool,
		execute: async (toolCallId, params, signal, onUpdate) => {
			const activeBefore = runner.getActiveTools();
			const result = await execute(toolCallId, params, signal, onUpdate);
			const activeAfter = runner.getActiveTools();
			if (!activeBefore.every((name) => activeAfter.includes(name))) return result;

			const beforeNames = new Set(activeBefore);
			const addedToolNames = activeAfter.filter((name) => !beforeNames.has(name));
			if (addedToolNames.length === 0) return result;
			return {

# Pi Extension API Overview

## Module Export Shape

Extensions export a default factory function receiving `ExtensionAPI`:

```typescript
import type { ExtensionAPI } from "@earendil-works/pi-coding-agent";

export default function (pi: ExtensionAPI) {
  // Subscribe to events, register tools, commands, etc.
}
```

The factory can be synchronous or async. If async, pi awaits completion before continuing startup.

## Language Requirement

**Extensions must be written in TypeScript.** The documentation states: *"Extensions are loaded via jiti, so TypeScript works without compilation."* This enables type safety and modern syntax while maintaining zero-compilation workflows.

## Core Registration Functions

| Function | Purpose |
|----------|---------|
| `pi.on(event, handler)` | Subscribe to lifecycle events |
| `pi.registerTool(definition)` | Register LLM-callable tools |
| `pi.registerCommand(name, options)` | Add slash commands like `/mycommand` |
| `pi.registerShortcut(keys, options)` | Bind keyboard shortcuts |
| `pi.registerFlag(name, options)` | Define CLI flags |
| `pi.registerProvider(name, config)` | Add or override model providers |

## Key Event Categories

- **Lifecycle:** `session_start`, `session_shutdown`, `project_trust`
- **Agent:** `before_agent_start`, `agent_start`, `agent_end`, `agent_settled`
- **Tools:** `tool_call`, `tool_result`, `tool_execution_start/up
… 1,076 more characters

WebFetch
input
{
 "url": "https://pi.dev/docs/latest/packages",
 "prompt": "How are Pi packages distributed and installed? What do they contain? How many community packages/extensions exist? What is the manifest format?"
}
result
# Pi Packages: Distribution, Installation, and Structure

## Distribution and Installation

Pi packages are distributed through three primary sources:

1. **npm**: Using specs like `npm:@scope/pkg@1.2.3`, installed to `~/.pi/agent/npm/` or `.pi/npm/`
2. **git**: Via repositories with refs like `git:github.com/user/repo@v1`, supporting HTTPS and SSH
3. **Local paths**: Direct filesystem references, either absolute or relative

Installation uses straightforward commands: `"pi install npm:@foo/bar@1.0.0"` or `"pi install git:github.com/user/repo@v1"`.

## Package Contents

Pi packages bundle four resource types:

- Extensions (TypeScript/JavaScript files)
- Skills (Markdown-based instructions)
- Prompt templates (Markdown files)
- Themes (JSON files)

## Manifest Format

Packages declare resources in `package.json` under a `"pi"` key:

```json
{
  "pi": {
    "extensions": ["./extensions"],
    "skills": ["./skills"],
    "prompts": ["./prompts"],
    "themes": ["./themes"]
  }
}
```

Alternatively, packages can use conventional directories (`extensions/`, `skills/`, `prompts/`, `themes/`) for automatic discovery.

## Community Packages

The documentation does not specify how many community packages exist currently.

=== native modules in TUI ===
import { createRequire } from "node:module";
import { dirname, join } from "node:path";
import { fileURLToPath } from "node:url";

const moduleRequire = createRequire(import.meta.url);
const TUI_PACKAGE_NAME = "@earendil-works/pi-tui";

export interface NativeModuleCandidateOptions {
	moduleUrl?: string;
	execPath?: string;
	resolvePackage?: (specifier: string) => string;
}

export function getNativeModuleCandidates(nativePath: string, options: NativeModuleCandidateOptions = {}): string[] {
	const moduleDir = dirname(fileURLToPath(options.moduleUrl ?? import.meta.url));
	const candidates: string[] = [];

	try {
		const packageEntry = (options.resolvePackage ?? moduleRequire.resolve)(TUI_PACKAGE_NAME);
		candidates.push(join(dirname(packageEntry), "..", nativePath));
	} catch {
		// Standalone binaries do not have an installed TUI package.
	}

	candidates.push(
		join(moduleDir, "..", nativePath),
		join(moduleDir, nativePath),
		join(dirname(options.execPath ?? process.execPath), nativePath),
	);
	return Array.from(new Set(candidates));

Claude

Done. The report is at doc/pi-dotnet10-feasibility-report.html.

The finding

Migrating to .NET 10 and keeping near-full plugin compatibility are mutually exclusive requirements. I cloned earendil-works/pi and read the extension subsystem rather than working from docs, and the reason is structural:

Pi's plugin contract is an in-process JavaScript object graph, not a wire protocol. loader.ts uses jiti to transpile extension TypeScript at runtime and execute it in the agent's own Node process. Handlers get live references — the TUI instance, SessionManager, ModelRegistry, an AbortSignal, callback functions — and are expected to mutate event.input in place. Extensions also import node:fs, node:child_process and arbitrary npm packages directly.

No C# program reproduces that without embedding Node.js — at which point the migration is nominal. I assessed all four JS-in-.NET mechanisms: Jint and ClearScript both fail outright (V8 is a JS engine; node:* modules are Node's, not V8's). Only node-api-dotnet and a Node sidecar work, and both keep Node in the runtime.

Recommendation: build a .NET 10 client on Pi's existing pi-protocol v1 seam (which Pi already ships and supports), running Pi unmodified as a sidecar. ~4–8 engineer-months against 55–85 for a rewrite, and it preserves 100% of the ecosystem.

Two things worth flagging about the brief itself: the monorepo publishes ten packages, not the three listed — 123,629 LOC production plus 115,921 LOC of tests. And I measured extension coupling empirically across Pi's 85 bundled extensions: 41% render into the TUI, which is the practical ceiling on any out-of-process bridge and the reason even the sidecar-based "true migration" (Option B) lands near 59% compatibility rather than "nearly full".

Deliverables

  • doc/pi-dotnet10-feasibility-report.html — 13 sections, 9 data tables, 5 inlined diagrams. Verified standalone: zero external references, no horizontal overflow, no broken anchors, print stylesheet included.
  • doc/diagrams/ — 5 SVGs, also usable independently
  • doc/icons/ — 31 SVGs using currentColor so they recolour in context

Diagram nodes use icons rather than blocks as asked — server, Azure, .NET, Node, plugin, container, Key Vault and so on.

Two issues caught and fixed during verification: sub-labels overflowing their cards in Figure 1, and code blocks collapsing to a single line (a div doesn't preserve whitespace — needed white-space:pre).

One caveat on the numbers: the effort figures are model-derived from measured line counts using a stated throughput assumption, and §7 shows the arithmetic so you can challenge it. They're planning ranges, not bottom-up estimates from a named team.

## Conversational Style

- Keep answers short and concise
- No emojis in commits, issues, PR comments, or code
- No fluff or cheerful filler text (e.g., "Thanks @user" not "Thanks so much @user!")

Claude

Kit is written. Here's how to proceed.

The shape of the problem

Two properties of Option A determine the delegation strategy:

  • Roughly 70% is mechanical translation with an existing test oracle — Codex's strongest case.
  • The extension host is a design task, not a port. core/extensions/ is 4,121 LOC you cannot translate, and ~56k LOC of pi-coding-agent is shaped by what replaces it. This gates the largest wave and is not delegatable.

Decide the extension API before anything else

Mirror Pi's ExtensionAPI in C# as closely as the language permits — same event names, same registration verbs, same context surface. Where C# can't follow, fix the pattern once: event.input mutation → a mutable args object; newSession({ setup, withSession }) → Func<>/Action<>; AbortSignal → CancellationToken.

You still can't run the TypeScript extensions. But a 1:1 mirror makes porting the 85 bundled extensions a mechanical, delegatable wave. An idiomatic-C# redesign makes each one a bespoke rewrite — at which point the ecosystem is genuinely gone rather than merely re-typed. This is the highest-leverage decision in the project and it's worth a week.

Oracles are what make delegation work

You cannot review 120k lines of generated C# by reading it. You need machine-checkable equivalence:

  • Port the upstream tests with each implementation. The 115,921-line vitest suite is the specification — treat it as the deliverable, not overhead.
  • Port faux.ts early (708 LOC). It's Pi's deterministic provider double and the upstream coding-agent suite is built on it. Every later agent-loop task needs it.
  • Record/replay HTTP fixtures for Pi.Ai; golden terminal buffers for Pi.Tui.
  • Cross-runtime conformance is free in wave 4 — because pi-protocol is a real wire protocol, your C# client can be tested against the unmodified TypeScript pi-server in CI before either side is done. Best defect-per-effort in the plan.

Build the harness before wave 2. Without it Codex produces plausible C# and you have no way to know if it's right.

About your clone

Two things:

  1. It's v0.84.4-1-g853a80d26 — one commit past the tag. Pin deliberately: git checkout v0.84.4, or accept that commit and record it.
  2. For Codex cloud, the reference must live inside the PiCSharp repo. Cloud tasks get your repository in a sandbox; a sibling at C:\MyProjects\pi won't exist there. Add it as a submodule so every packet can cite reference/pi/... paths:
git submodule add https://github.com/earendil-works/pi reference/pi && git -C reference/pi checkout v0.84.4

Your local clone stays useful for Codex CLI runs and your own inspection.

Start here

  1. D1 and D2 by hand — extension API and solution layout. Nothing delegates cleanly until target paths are fixed.
  2. T0.1 scaffold, then T0.2 differential harness.
  3. T1.1 / T1.2 / T1.3 in parallel — three small independent tasks whose real purpose is calibration: measure how much review each Codex PR actually costs you before committing to wave 2.

One planning note

Heavy Codex use plausibly takes the 55–85 engineer-month figure down to roughly 25–40, but the bottleneck moves rather than disappearing: it becomes review throughput. Design work and behavioural-parity chasing don't compress. Staff reviewers, not just packet authors, and hold work-in-flight to about five unreviewed PRs.

Files

  • codex/AGENTS.md — copy to repo root when you scaffold; Codex reads it from there. Modelled on Pi's own, with the "TypeScript source is the specification" rule as the prime directive.
  • codex/work-breakdown.md — every wave and task with measured LOC, dependency order, and A/B/C delegation ratings.
  • codex/task-template.md — the packet format, with the standing rules that keep parallel runs from colliding.

The work breakdown reflects the provider correction: Pi.Ai slices into ~11 protocol ports plus one trivial task for all 47 provider declarations, not 47 separate integrations.

Claude

The delegation kit isn't enough — but the gap isn't traditional specs. It's six documents the kit already names and I didn't write. AGENTS.md and the work breakdown both reference a docs/ directory that doesn't exist yet.

Skip the functional spec

You already have one, and it's better than anything you could write: 123,629 lines of working TypeScript plus 115,921 lines of tests that pin the behaviour precisely.

Writing a prose functional spec for Pi would mean transcribing executable behaviour into a lossy, unverifiable format — then maintaining it against a source that already says the same thing exactly. Worse, it would create a second source of truth that directly contradicts the prime rule in AGENTS.md ("the TS source wins"). When Codex hits a conflict between your prose and the TS, you've made the situation ambiguous where it was previously decidable.

This is the property that makes a port different from greenfield work. Greenfield needs a functional spec because nobody knows what the software should do. Here, everybody does — including the agent, which can read it.

Write design docs only where you diverge

A port needs design work at exactly the points where there is no TS answer to copy. In this project that set is small and already enumerated as D1–D5 in the work breakdown:

DocumentWhy it existsGates
docs/extension-api.mdThe redesign. No TS counterpart to copy.Wave 6, and all of wave 7
docs/solution-layout.mdEvery task packet cites target pathsEvery packet
docs/differential-testing.mdThe oracle strategyWaves 2–6
docs/session-format.mdCompatibility decisionT6.2
docs/tui-strategy.mdPort vs. Spectre.ConsoleWave 5
docs/dependencies.mdNuGet approval listOngoing

Plus one I should have called out and didn't: docs/translation-patterns.md. Forty-odd parallel Codex tasks will each independently decide how to render a TS discriminated union, undefined vs null, AbortSignal, streaming deltas, and error hierarchies — unless you fix each once, centrally. Inconsistency across independently-generated PRs is the top quality risk in delegated porting, and no traditional artifact covers it. AGENTS.md gestures at a few of these; they deserve their own document with worked examples.

The implementation plan you already have

work-breakdown.md is the engineering implementation plan: dependency-ordered, sized from measured LOC, with parallelism and acceptance criteria. What it deliberately omits is the management layer — schedule, staffing, budget, go/no-go gates. Write that only if you need to fund or coordinate the work; it won't change what Codex produces.

The heuristic

For delegated work, the test for whether a document is worth writing is: does it change what the agent outputs?

  • Functional spec → no, the agent reads the TS → skip
  • Translation patterns → yes, it's the difference between 40 consistent PRs and 40 divergent ones → write
  • Extension API → yes, it's the only spec for code with no TS counterpart → write first
  • Gantt chart → no → write only for humans who need it

That heuristic will also stop you writing the other twelve documents a traditional process would demand.

Two of these are on the critical path and I can draft them properly: docs/extension-api.md as a decision-ready proposal with concrete C# signatures mirrored against the real ExtensionAPI surface, and docs/translation-patterns.md with worked TS→C# examples pulled from the actual source. The other four are mostly your decisions to record rather than mine to propose. Want me to draft those two?

Claude

All seven now exist and every cross-reference resolves.

What was missing, and now isn't

DocumentDecision it records
docs/extension-api.mdMirror Pi's ExtensionAPI — 37 events, all registration verbs, with fixed resolutions for the five things C# can't do directly
docs/solution-layout.mdProject graph, target paths for every packet, Directory.Build.props
docs/differential-testing.mdFive oracles, in cost-effectiveness order
docs/session-format.mdStay byte-compatible with Pi's JSONL tree format
docs/tui-strategy.mdPort pi-tui; don't adopt Terminal.Gui's widget model
docs/dependencies.mdnpm→NuGet mapping, what's replaced by the BCL, what to port instead
docs/translation-patterns.mdThe 14 TS→C# patterns, fixed once

They're written as proposals with recommendations, not blank templates — you can disagree with any of them, but there's something specific to disagree with.

Three things worth knowing before you sign off

D1 and D5 are one decision. The mirror only works if Pi.Tui preserves upstream's component contracts. Adopt Terminal.Gui's View hierarchy and tier-2 of the extension API can't be mirrored, so the 41% of UI-coupled extensions become bespoke rewrites — and D1's justification collapses. If you reverse the TUI choice, reopen the extension API in the same meeting.

Session compatibility is nearly free and worth taking. The format is JSONL — a header line plus an append-only tree of nine entry types. Two traps: version is absent in v1 sessions (don't default it), and you must preserve unknown fields via [JsonExtensionData] or opening a newer session silently destroys data.

Usage.reasoning is the pattern to internalise. Upstream's own comment says it's "set to a number (possibly 0) by providers that expose a reasoning breakdown; left undefined by providers that don't." 0 and undefined mean different things. A port that collapses them corrupts cost reporting in a way no unit test will catch. That single distinction is why §1 of the patterns doc leads with undefined vs null.

One correction to yesterday's kit

dependencies.md says not to add a package for partial JSON parsing, which is right — but I found upstream's utils/json-parse.ts pairs partial-json with a repairJson fallback for malformed provider output. A plain resumable Utf8JsonReader reproduces only the happy path. The patterns doc now says to port that file in full, repair path included.

Where you stand

The kit tells Codex how to work; these tell it what to build where the TypeScript has no answer. Together that's the complete spec set — no functional spec, no traditional design spec.

Still outstanding before dispatch: sign-off on D1 and D5 (they're coupled), and T0.1/T0.2 by hand. The acceptance test in extension-api.md §7 — hand-port permission-gate.ts, todo.ts and status-line.ts against the draft API — is the cheapest way to find out whether D1 holds, and I'd run it before committing to wave 6.

Prompt 92026-08-29 07:47:05

YOu still have some tokens. But are steps forward?

Claude

Yes — the critical path is clear, and the top item is something I can do right now.

Where things stand

Everything so far is analysis and specification. Nothing is validated. Per my own extension-api.md §7, the entire plan rests on one unproven assumption: that the mirrored C# API is faithful enough for extensions to port mechanically. If that's wrong, D1 is wrong, wave 7 evaporates, and the TUI strategy (D5) changes with it.

The next steps, in order:

#StepGates
1Validate D1 — hand-port 3 extensions against the draft APID5, wave 6, wave 7
2Sign off D1 + D5 togetherT0.1
3T0.1 scaffold — solution, CI, reference/ submoduleevery packet

================ permission-gate.ts ================
/**
 * Permission Gate Extension
 *
 * Prompts for confirmation before running potentially dangerous bash commands.
 * Patterns checked: rm -rf, sudo, chmod/chown 777
 */

import type { ExtensionAPI } from "@earendil-works/pi-coding-agent";

export default function (pi: ExtensionAPI) {
	const dangerousPatterns = [/\brm\s+(-rf?|--recursive)/i, /\bsudo\b/i, /\b(chmod|chown)\b.*777/i];

	pi.on("tool_call", async (event, ctx) => {
		if (event.toolName !== "bash") return undefined;

		const command = event.input.command as string;
		const isDangerous = dangerousPatterns.some((p) => p.test(command));

		if (isDangerous) {
			if (!ctx.hasUI) {
				// In non-interactive mode, block by default
				return { block: true, reason: "Dangerous command blocked (no UI for confirmation)" };
			}

			const choice = await ctx.ui.select(`⚠️ Dangerous command:\n\n  ${command}\n\nAllow?`, ["Yes", "No"]);

			if (choice !== "Yes") {
				return { block: true, reason: "Blocked by user" };
			}
		}

		return undefined;
	});
}

/**
 * Status Line Extension
 *
 * Demonstrates ctx.ui.setStatus() for displaying persistent status text in the footer.
 * Shows turn progress with themed colors.
 */

import type { ExtensionAPI } from "@earendil-works/pi-coding-agent";

export default function (pi: ExtensionAPI) {
	let turnCount = 0;

	pi.on("session_start", async (_event, ctx) => {
		const theme = ctx.ui.theme;
		ctx.ui.setStatus("status-demo", theme.fg("dim", "Ready"));
	});

	pi.on("turn_start", async (_event, ctx) => {
		turnCount++;
		const theme = ctx.ui.theme;
		const spinner = theme.fg("accent", "●");
		const text = theme.fg("dim", ` Turn ${turnCount}...`);
		ctx.ui.setStatus("status-demo", spinner + text);
	});

	pi.on("turn_end", async (_event, ctx) => {
		const theme = ctx.ui.theme;
		const check = theme.fg("success", "✓");
		const text = theme.fg("dim", ` Turn ${turnCount} complete`);
		ctx.ui.setStatus("status-demo", check + text);
	});
}

## main...origin/main
Claude

Pushed as 01f2f7c. Step 1 is done, and it earned its keep.

D1 passes — but it disproved something I wrote

All three extensions ported cleanly. The design holds. What it also did was falsify a claim I'd made in the feasibility report and two design docs.

I asserted that UI-coupled extensions "own a region of the render tree." They don't. The actual contract in packages/tui/src/tui.ts is four members:

export interface Component {
  render(width: number): string[];
  handleInput?(data: string): void;
  wantsKeyRelease?: boolean;
  invalidate?(): void;
}

Extensions emit arrays of pre-styled strings. They never touch the diff algorithm, never hold a retained widget tree, never do cursor math. ctx.ui.setWidget even accepts a bare string[].

Three consequences:

  1. D1 is safer than I claimed. Tier-2 UI is not a risk tier — that interface maps to C# with no loss. Downgraded.
  2. D5's central argument was wrong. "A foreign widget model breaks the extension mirror" is false; extensions never see a widget model. Porting pi-tui is still right, but on narrower grounds: upstream's own 43 interactive components are written against pi-tui internals, extensions import matchesKey/Text/truncateToWidth directly, and golden-buffer byte-identity is harder through a foreign abstraction. It's now a cost argument, not an impossibility one — so a Terminal.Gui proposal deserves a hearing rather than a veto.
  3. Three API amendments, all folded in: sync handler overloads (otherwise all 85 ports end every handler with return ValueTask.CompletedTask;), a non-generic CustomAsync (no Task<void> in C#), and source-generated tool parameter schemas.

The only structural translation across 363 lines was tool parameter schemas — typebox builds them as runtime values. Confirmed as the project's top schema risk: the C# emitter must match typebox's JSON byte for byte or provider behaviour shifts.

One thing needing your call

The committed feasibility report carries the same overstatement. Diagram 2 marks custom TUI components as not crossing a process boundary, and Option B's "~59% compatibility" rests on it. Since render(width) => string[] is essentially a pure function, those components could cross a boundary — the real cost is per-frame IPC during streaming, which is a latency problem, not a correctness one. Option B was more viable than I represented.

That doesn't reopen your decision — you rejected B and C for reasons this doesn't touch — but the report is the artifact of record and it's wrong on this point. Regenerating means redoing diagram 2, diagram 3 and the HTML. Say the word and I'll do it.

Next

  • Round 2 acceptance test — modal-editor.ts, rainbow-editor.ts, custom-provider-anthropic/. These cover EditorFactory, onTerminalInput and registerProvider, the highest-variance surfaces round 1 never reached. Cheap now, expensive in month nine.
  • Then D1/D5 sign-off, T0.1 scaffold, T0.2 harness.

I'd run round 2 next unless you'd rather I fix the report first.

Two notes on the name, neither blocking:

  • It's Á (U+00C1) + I, exactly as you wrote it. If you meant plain AI, say so and it's a one-line git mv.
  • Non-ASCII directory names in git are fine but occasionally awkward — git quotes it as "\303\201I/" in stat output, and file:// URLs need %C3%81. Nothing broke; just worth knowing if CI or tooling touches that path later.

			return;

			super.handleInput(data);

## main...origin/main
Claude

Round 2 done and pushed as 137a680. D1 passes both rounds — five amendments, no redesign.

What round 2 found

All three ported mechanically, but one contract is bigger than round 1 suggested.

Editors are subclassed, not implemented. Both editor extensions do this:

class ModalEditor extends CustomEditor {
  handleInput(data) { /* … */ super.handleInput(data); }
  render(width)     { const lines = super.render(width); /* decorate */ return lines; }
}

And CustomEditor extends Editor — pi-tui's. So an extension inherits, transitively, the whole editor: text buffer, cursor, autocomplete, undo stack, kill-ring, word navigation. That's a far larger surface than the four-member IComponent from round 1.

Still mirrorable — C# has inheritance, virtual, protected — and both ports were line-for-line. But it forces Amendment D: Pi.Tui.Editor and CustomEditor must be public, non-sealed, with virtual HandleInput/Render, and their protected surface is part of the versioned extension contract. A porter can't pick member visibility on convenience grounds; the visibility is the contract.

Blast radius is narrow: 3 of 85 extensions replace the editor (3.5%), not the 41% that touch UI generally.

Custom providers port cleanly. registerProvider is a declarative object plus four delegates. Only friction is Amendment E: streamSimple returns a push stream written from a detached task, so it needs a Channel<T>-backed equivalent — not async IAsyncEnumerable with yield, which would change provider error and cancellation timing.

Knock-on effects

tui-strategy.md gets a stronger lead argument. Reason (1) is no longer "extensions import pi-tui helpers" — it's now "extensions inherit from pi-tui's Editor," which is a hard break under a foreign widget model rather than a degradation. The other reasons shifted down one.

That also resolves the open question I'd flagged: D5's "reconsider if round 2 fails" clause. It didn't fail — but reason (1) got harder to answer on Terminal.Gui, not easier.

One thing I did not do

Report rev 2 says editors "degrade over IPC" under Option B. For editor-replacing extensions that's too generous — super.Render() is a base-class call into the host, so it doesn't degrade across a process boundary, it doesn't work. rainbow-editor.ts compounds it with a 60 ms animation timer calling requestRender(), roughly 17 IPC round trips a second.

The ~90% headline survives since the affected population is 3.5%. I recorded the nuance in the spike rather than triggering a rev 3 for one word — say if you'd rather I make it exact.

Where the critical path stands

D1 and D5 are now evidence-backed and ready for your sign-off. After that: T0.1 (solution scaffold, CI, reference/ submodule pinned to v0.84.4) and T0.2 (differential harness) — both human-led, then wave 1's three calibration packets.

Claude

MTP treats "zero tests ran" as a failure — correct behaviour, but the scaffold has no tests yet. Setting a default argument rather than adding filler tests.

Bash
input
{
 "command": "cd \"C:/MyProjects/PiCSharp\"\npython - <<'PY'\nimport io\np=r\"C:/MyProjects/PiCSharp/tests/Directory.Build.props\"\ns=io.open(p,encoding=\"utf-8\").read()\ns=s.replace(\"\"\"    <UseMicrosoftTestingPlatformRunner>true</UseMicrosoftTestingPlatformRunner>\"\"\",\n\"\"\"    <UseMicrosoftTestingPlatformRunner>true</UseMicrosoftTestingPlatformRunner>\n    <!-- SCAFFOLD ONLY. Claude

T0.1 done and pushed (19e1d70). The scaffold is real and verified, not just written:

Build:  21 projects, 0 warnings, 0 errors (warnings-as-errors on)
Format: clean
Tests:  15 passed, 0 failed
Ref:    reference/pi pinned at v0.84.4

12 libraries + pi CLI + 9 test projects, wired to the exact dependency graph in solution-layout.md. Upstream is a submodule pinned to the v0.84.4 tag — note that's the tag, not the +1 commit your clone sits on — and CI fails if that pin moves.

Building it corrected three things the docs had wrong

This is why scaffolding by hand was worth it — each of these would have broken a Codex packet for reasons unrelated to its actual task:

  1. .NET 10 emits .slnx, the XML solution format, not .sln.
  1. AnalysisLevel=latest-all was a bad call of mine. It turns on rules that actively fight a faithful port — CA1002 (exposed lists), CA1707 (underscored test names, which mirror upstream), CA2007 (ConfigureAwait), CA1848 (logging delegates). With warnings-as-errors, every one is a build break on style, and in a delegated port that means packets failing for the wrong reasons. Dropped to latest-recommended, with each deliberately relaxed rule listed and justified in .editorconfig.
  1. xunit.v3 runs on Microsoft.Testing.Platform, not VSTest. Needs the runner opt-in in global.json (an MSBuild property genuinely does not work — I tried), OutputType=Exe on test projects, and -- --filter-not-trait instead of --filter. Microsoft.NET.Test.Sdk and xunit.runner.visualstudio aren't needed at all.

All three are corrected in solution-layout.md, dependencies.md and AGENTS.md, so nobody re-derives them.

Two judgement calls

The ProjectReferenceTests aren't filler. MTP treats "zero tests ran" as a failure, which is correct — and --minimum-expected-tests 0 is rejected outright. Rather than Assert.True(true), each test loads its referenced assemblies. That fails loudly on a mis-wired reference instead of letting it surface as a baffling error inside the first ported test. Worth keeping.

.skip-budget is at 0, enforced by a CI job that lists offenders. Raising it requires a written justification in the same PR — the mechanism from differential-testing.md for catching packets marked done with the hard half quietly skipped.

Next

T0.2 — the differential harness: normalisers, HTTP fixture recorder, replay runner, golden-buffer capture. It's the gate on wave 2, roughly two weeks of work, and my own docs say build it before any porting starts. That's what I'd do next.

Worth flagging: D1 and D5 are still marked Proposed. Both now have evidence behind them, and nothing so far depends on your sign-off — but wave 6 does, and T0.2 is the last work that doesn't.

The submodule discrepancy turned out to be benign, and worth knowing: v0.84.4 is a lightweight tag, so plain git describe skips it and reports the nearest annotated tag instead (v0.0.2-5566-g…). The pin itself is exact — HEAD and v0.84.4 both resolve to b79e4cc83, and the CI check passes because it uses --tags --exact-match. If you ever see that v0.0.2-… string in submodule output, it's cosmetic, not drift.

Nothing outstanding on my side. T0.2 — the differential harness is next whenever you want it: normalisers, HTTP fixture recorder, replay runner, golden-buffer capture. It's the gate on wave 2, and the last piece of work before any porting starts.

Claude

CI is green. Both jobs, every step, on d18533e.

Two real bugs, both mine

1. The pin check was wrong, not the pin (b9fbe25)

actions/checkout fetches submodules shallowly, so reference/pi arrives with zero tags. My git describe --tags --exact-match could never succeed there — it fell through to UNPINNED and killed the job before anything built. I reproduced it locally: a --depth 1 clone of pi has 0 tags.

Now it compares commit SHAs, which are exact and survive a shallow checkout. The expected tag and SHA live in reference/PINNED, so bumping the upstream reference means editing the submodule pointer and that file in one commit — the bump stays visible in review, which was the point of the gate.

I also switched setup-dotnet to global-json-file. dotnet-version: 10.0.x can resolve to a lower SDK feature band than global.json requires, which would have failed later with a confusing "compatible SDK not found."

2. Line endings (d18533e)

.editorconfig demanded CRLF; the Linux runner checks out LF; dotnet format wanted to rewrite every file. Passed locally, failed on CI — the classic shape of this bug.

This is the CRLF issue I flagged two turns back and offered to fix. I should have just fixed it then; leaving it cost a red build. The repo is now LF everywhere via .gitattributes (text=auto eol=lf), .editorconfig asks for lf, and the tree is renormalised. That also silences the CRLF warnings git was emitting on every add.

Verified after renormalising: Release build clean, format passes, 15 tests pass.

State

d18533e  Fix CI: normalise line endings to LF repo-wide      ← CI green
b9fbe25  Fix CI: verify the upstream pin by commit SHA
19e1d70  T0.1: scaffold the .NET 10 solution

The scaffold is now genuinely validated end-to-end on a clean Linux machine, not just on mine — which is the thing that actually matters before delegating packets to Codex.

T0.2 (differential harness) remains next, and D1/D5 still await your sign-off before wave 6.

Claude

Packet is committed at ÍmplementationKit/packets/T1.2-pi-protocol.md.

Start with T1.2, not T1.1 — I had that wrong

Writing the packet made me inspect pi-telemetry, and my "A / excellent fit" rating was wrong. Its index.ts is 29 type-only declarations against 2 runtime functions, with 11 uses of conditional and mapped types (InferStartAttributes<T>) that derive attribute types from a schema value at compile time. C# generics can't express that — someone has to first decide how schema-derived typing is represented. That's a design task, not a translation. Re-rated C and pushed.

Pi.Protocol is the right first delegation: 1,236 LOC, 53 schemas, zero conditional types, real upstream tests, byte-verifiable. Merging it also switches on the cross-runtime conformance oracle, the cheapest defect detection on the project.

The prompt

Point Codex at the repo and give it this — the packet is the prompt:

codex "Complete the task in ÍmplementationKit/packets/T1.2-pi-protocol.md. Read AGENTS.md and docs/translation-patterns.md first. The TypeScript in reference/pi is the specification and is read-only. Write only to the target paths named in the packet; if you need anything outside them, stop and explain in your summary instead of doing it."

For Codex cloud, use the same text as the task description — it reads AGENTS.md from the repo root automatically, which is why moving it there mattered.

The packet carries the real detail: exact source files, writable paths, three oracles, and eight hazards. The one most likely to sink a naive port — upstream ships its own 384-line CBOR codec, and dependencies.md lists System.Formats.Cbor as the replacement on assumption, not proof. The packet requires byte-identical output be demonstrated before that substitution is allowed. Get that wrong and you get a wire-incompatible client that passes all its own tests.

How to run the first one

Send one task, not three. Wave 1 exists to calibrate. From this first PR, measure: how long review actually took, how many review cycles, whether hazards were respected, whether tests were ported or quietly skipped. That number sets your parallelism for wave 2's thirteen tasks.

Then batch T1.3 (faux provider) with the first wave-2 tasks, and schedule T1.1 only after someone makes the telemetry typing decision.

Two things to watch in the returned PR:

  • Skips. CI enforces a skip budget of zero, so a skipped test is a red build — but check the PR narrative too, for tests quietly not ported at all.
  • Scope. Any edit outside src/Pi.Protocol/ and tests/Pi.Protocol.Tests/ is a packet violation worth pushing back on, even if the change looks reasonable. Parallel tasks depend on that discipline holding.

Still open on the critical path: T0.2 (differential harness) before wave 2, and your sign-off on D1/D5 before wave 6.
